## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 13d: Relay Choir's record accepted anything, and guarding it uncovered a load-order trap

Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the stages, the pads, the gates and the scoring are unchanged.

- **The stored best took anything.** `-5`, `12.7` and `1e24` all reached the Best pill verbatim, and `1e24` printed as "1e+24" - a record no run could beat. Now required whole, non-negative and capped.
- **The first version of that guard broke the page, and only for one kind of value.** It read a cap declared beside it, but the guard runs while the state object is still being built, before any const at that point exists. A negative or fractional value returned earlier in the function and never touched the cap, so the crash hid behind exactly the inputs the guard handles first - only an absurdly large value reached the line that failed. Moving the cap inside the function fixed it, and all seven stored-value cases now load cleanly.
- Checked and already correct: the board already marks which targets are being hit and which are not, the guidance already names how many sources or gates are still needed, and the animation loop legitimately runs because the sequencer is always playing.
- Verified: seven stored-value cases (negative, fractional, absurd, junk, unparseable, null and a real record) each loading with no page error and the right value; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0) / audit:contrast (0 findings); npm test 56/56; test:corrupt-storage across all 100 games (clean on re-run after one known transient); git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 13c: Market Minute named the card about to hit and would not say what it did

Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the goods, the eight-card demand deck, the contracts and the scoring are unchanged.

- **The game told you the name of the next card and withheld the description it already had for it.** Ending a turn read "End to Factory Rush", and every card in the deck carries a written line like "Assembly lines chase chips and ore" that was never shown for the card you were about to face. Naming a card without saying what it does is a memory test, not a decision - especially in an eight-turn game where buying before a rise is the whole point.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 13b: Circuit Putt's aim line showed an eighth of the putt

Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the three holes, the walls, bumpers, sand and physics constants are unchanged.

- **The aim line was six to eight times too short.** It was a straight ray of `58 + power * 88` pixels, a formula touching neither the launch speed nor the friction that decides the roll. Measured across a sweep of real putts: it drew 109 to 146 pixels while the ball rolled 694 to 1015. In a game whose holes are built from rails, bumpers and sand, the indicator also never showed which of them the ball would meet.
- **The preview is now the putt.** It steps a copy of the ball through the game's own resolvers - the same bounds bounce, the same wall and bumper handlers, the same sand and green friction, the same cup radius - draws the real curving path, rings where the ball will stop, and turns gold and says "IN THE CUP" when the putt drops.
- **Sharing the rules was not enough; the stepping had to match too.** A fixed slice diverged from the real roll by up to 166 pixels on putts with several bounces, because the game varies its substep count with speed. Once the forecast stepped exactly as the game steps, the worst resting-place error across eighteen putts fell inside a few pixels and every predicted hole-out really sank.
- **A stroke record with no floor.** This is golf, so fewer strokes is better and the impossible value is a small one. The guard only asked that the number be finite and above zero, so a stored total of **1 stroke for the whole course** was accepted and no round could ever beat it. The floor is now one putt per hole, which is a hole in one everywhere, and anything below it is rejected.
- Verified: 29-check Playwright probe (an eighteen-putt forecast-versus-reality sweep across aim and power, hole-out predictions checked both ways, resting place and roll distance against the real ball, a fifty-call no-side-effects check, canvas pixel sampling proving the path is painted, seven impossible stroke records plus a plausible one, fresh-page determinism, both media settings, page weight, and a check that the old ray formula is gone); HEAD-first confirmation before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshot read) / audit:contrast (0 findings); npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 13a: Rhythm Circuit punished one late press twice and drew its timing bands almost invisibly

Batch 13 opens. Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the chart, the lanes, the scoring curve and the judged windows are unchanged.

- **A 25 millisecond gap where a lane holding a note said the lane was empty.** A note stopped being hittable at 165ms late, but did not count as missed until 190ms. In between, pressing that lane answered "Empty lane", broke the combo and played the miss sound - and then the note aged out and counted a miss as well. Measured on the shipped page: a note 167ms late, pressed, reported "Empty", and the miss count went up anyway. One late press, punished twice, for a note that was already doomed.
- **Three numbers that were meant to agree, written in three places.** The judged windows, the painted bands and the auto-miss threshold were separate literals. They now come from one block, so a note stops being hittable at exactly the moment it misses. Verified across a whole run: there is no longer any moment where a pending note cannot be hit, and a press at the very edge of the window still scores a hit and counts no miss.
- **The bands were painted at an alpha nobody could see.** Measured against bare lane brightness of 42, the outer band read 46 and the middle one 53 - a difference of four and eleven steps. The whole purpose of the bands is to show how much room a press has. They now read 72, 101 and 142 against the same bare lane, with edge lines so the boundary between bands is a place rather than a gradient.
- **The stored best had no ceiling.** Finite-and-non-negative let `1e24` through, since it floors to itself and printed as "1e+24". Capped now.
- Verified: 24-check Playwright probe (window-versus-miss agreement, an edge-of-window press scoring a hit with no miss, a full-run sweep finding zero unhittable moments, canvas pixel sampling of all three bands against bare lane, twelve real presses whose judgement and reported offset match the stated windows, 4 junk plus 1 valid storage seed, fresh-page determinism, idle frames, page weight, and a check that no stray timing literal remains); HEAD-first confirmation of all three defects before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshot read) / audit:contrast (0 findings); test:best-score-guard; test:realtime-progression; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 12f: Patchwork Foundry had a function to answer its only question and never called it

BATCH 12 COMPLETE (pinball-foundry, cipher-rooms, wordle, vector-pool, neon-drift, patchwork-foundry). Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the five boards, the plates, the vents, the ports and the budgets are unchanged.

- **The forecast was written and never used.** `connectionStats(extra)` takes an `extra` argument for exactly one purpose: answering "what would this placement connect?" All four call sites passed nothing. So a player spent moves from a fixed budget with no way to know what a move would buy, while the code to tell them sat one argument away.
- **Now every aim states what it would achieve.** The readout gives the ports before and after ("ports 2/4 to 3/4 (+1)"), how many vents the placement would block, how many moves would remain, and it says outright when a move completes the stage or when it is your last move and would not finish. All of it comes from the same breadth-first connectivity search that judges the real placement, so the promise cannot drift from the result.
- **Proved exact, not just plausible.** Every square on the board, at every rotation, was predicted and then actually placed: **zero mismatches between the predicted port count and the real one**, across both legal placements and refused ones.
- **Proved the promise is never a lie.** A search that plays only by the forecast found a winning line for the opening stage and replayed it on a fresh board to a solved stage, and across 220 attempted lines every single move the forecast called stage-completing really did complete it.
- **Proved side-effect free.** The forecast runs on a copy, so calling it forty times leaves the board byte-identical - a preview sharing mutable state with the real simulation is the standard way this fix goes wrong.
- **The stored best took anything.** `Infinity`, `-5`, `12.7` and `1e24` all reached the Best pill verbatim. Now required whole, non-negative and capped.
- **A layout regression the scoped check missed.** The scoring text was first added above the table, which pushed Vector Pool's Shoot button to y=597 on a phone - past the buried threshold - and the deploy gate caught it. The scoped capture run after that same edit had reported no issue, so the scoped form did not merely give weaker evidence, it disagreed outright with the full one. The text moved below the controls, where it cannot push the primary action down, and the full catalog-wide capture then passed locally before pushing.
- Verified: 27-check Playwright probe (a full board sweep of predictions versus real placements, refusal wording, the ports-and-moves readout, a 220-attempt solvability search with the winning line replayed on a fresh board, completion-prediction honesty, a 40-call no-side-effects check, 6 junk storage seeds, fresh-page determinism, page weight, and a check that the preview call site now exists); HEAD-first confirmation before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:corrupt-storage (100 games); npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean. Checked and already correct: refusals were already named per reason, and the loop already parks.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 12e: Neon Drift timed every lap and showed you none of them

Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the track, the physics constants, the gates and the three-lap structure are unchanged.

- **A best-time guard written for the wrong direction.** Every stored-best guard in this catalog caps the top, because in every other game a higher number is better. This is a time trial, where **lower is better, so the absurd value is a tiny one** - and the guard only asked that the number be finite and above zero. A stored best of `1e-300` was accepted, displayed as "0.00", and could never be beaten by any real race. Measured on the shipped page.
- **The floor comes from the track, not from a guess.** A race cannot be quicker than driving the whole circuit flat out, so the guard now rejects anything below the centreline length times three laps divided by the boosted top speed - 7.51 seconds on this track - and anything implausibly slow. Verified by recomputing that figure independently from the track literal in the source and matching it to the number the page publishes.
- **The game timed every lap and threw the numbers away.** It computed your last lap and mentioned it once in a status line that the very next checkpoint overwrote. In a three-lap time trial, your lap time is the whole point. There are now four clocks: this lap running live, your last lap, your best lap of the race, and your best race ever - and the last-lap pill turns green when the lap you just finished was the quickest one. Verified across a full autopiloted race that the pills match the diagnostics to within a hundredth of a second and that the best lap is always the quickest lap seen.
- **The boost economy was invisible.** Boost lifts top speed from 540 to 660, drains about three times faster than it refills, and only refills while you are not boosting - so a full bar is roughly two seconds of boost against about seven to earn it back. That trade is the main decision in the game and appeared nowhere. It is now written out, along with the fact that leaving the track sheds roughly five times as much speed as staying on it.
- **A load-order trap caught on the way in, and worth recording.** The new guard first reached for a `const` declared beside it, which crashed the page with "cannot access before initialization" - the guard runs while the state object is still being built, before any const or let at that point exists. Moving the work inside hoisted functions fixed it. A `let` cache had exactly the same problem, so the fix is functions, not variables.
- Verified: 31-check Playwright probe (6 impossible stored bests plus 1 plausible one, the floor cross-checked against an independent recomputation from the track points, a complete autopiloted three-lap race with per-lap pill-versus-diagnostics agreement, best-lap monotonicity, the banked race time checked against the physical floor, all three help statements, fresh-page determinism, both media settings, idle frames, page weight); HEAD-first confirmation before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:reduced-motion (52 games); npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 12d: Vector Pool's aim line stopped a third of the way along the shot

Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the table, the four stages, the physics constants and the payouts are unchanged.

- **The aim line had no relationship to the shot.** It was a straight dashed ray of `118 + power * 270` pixels, a formula that touches neither the launch speed nor the friction that actually decides how far the ball rolls. Measured across a sweep of real aims: the ray drew 240px while the ball rolled 348 to 666px, and at full power it draws 388px against a real roll of roughly 2,300px. The direction was right; the length was fiction, and the error grows with power - exactly when a player most needs to know.
- **It also never showed what the shot would hit,** which in a pool game is the only question that matters.
- **The preview is now the shot, traced through the same rules the shot obeys** - the same launch speed, the same friction curve, the same 0.9 rail damping, the same pocket radius. It draws the path to the first contact solid, rings that contact and names it ("1 WORTH 100", "SCRATCH", "RAIL 2"), dashes the roll-on afterwards so a certainty reads differently from a continuation, and outlines where the cue ball comes to rest.
- **The forecast is verified against reality, not just drawn.** Over 24 shots spanning aim and power: every predicted ball contact really struck that ball, every shot predicted to touch nothing left every ball still, the predicted resting place matched the real one, and the predicted roll distance tracked the real roll. The forecast is also published in the diagnostics, so it is testable rather than only visible.
- **Forecasting is free of side effects.** It steps a copy, so calling it forty times in a row leaves the table byte-identical - checked, because a preview that shares mutable state with the simulation is the classic way this fix goes wrong.
- **The scoring rules were nowhere.** A potted ball pays its own value plus 8 for every stroke left plus, if the cue ball touched a rail first, 60 and another 25 per rail up to five; a scratch costs 40. None of that appeared on screen - the table said only "Bank x0". The rules are now written out in the page text, and the table shows the live value of the bank you have built and the stroke bonus you would collect.
- **The stored best had no ceiling.** The guard checked only that the value was finite, so `1e24` passed straight through and printed as "1e+24" - an unbeatable record. Capped now.

## 2026-08-27 Claude - overhaul campaign batch 12c: Lexica could hand you an unwinnable round, and kept no record of anything

Targeted pass, not a rewrite - the word lists, the colouring rule and the six-guess structure are unchanged.

- **A saved game was restored without checking it was playable.** `load()` accepted any valid-JSON save whose `COLS` matched, so a stored answer of "zzzzz" was adopted verbatim. That word is not in the dictionary, so it can never be submitted - measured: typing the stored answer is refused with "Not in dictionary", meaning the round could not be won or lost, only abandoned by hand. Restores are now refused unless the answer is a word the game would itself accept as a guess, every stored guess is a real dictionary word, the guess count is within six, and the partial entry is plain letters. Seven kinds of unsound save proved refused, and a sound save still resumes to the exact guess and partial word.
- **Hard mode was quietly optional whenever a letter repeated.** The rule checked `current.includes(letter)` - presence only - so a single E satisfied two revealed Es. It now counts: a guess must carry at least as many of a letter as the most any single earlier guess revealed. Proved with the answer GEESE: after SHEER reveals two Es, STEAL keeps the green E in square three and is still correctly refused.
- **Both hard-mode refusals now name what is wrong.** "Hard mode: reuse revealed letters" became "Hard mode: use 2 of the letter E", and "keep green letters in place" became "Hard mode: square 3 must stay E".
- **Finishing a game left no trace at all.** Every other game in the catalog keeps a best; a word game kept nothing - no wins, no streak, no history. It now keeps games played, wins, current streak, best streak and a six-bucket guess distribution, shown as header pills and as a bar chart on the result card with your finishing row highlighted. Verified across a win, a reload, and a six-guess loss that resets the streak while keeping the best.
- **The new record is guarded like a score.** Six kinds of corrupt stats - junk, negative, wins above games played, fractional, an overflowing streak, a wrong-length distribution - are all sanitised into a self-consistent record rather than displayed.
- **Joins the reduced-motion gate, 51 games to 52.** The file has no media query of its own, but all of its motion is CSS keyframes (tile flip, row shake, input pulse) which the shared runtime reset collapses to about zero under reduce - measured at 1e-05s. It now reports the live state, so that dependency on the shared reset is a tested fact instead of an assumption.
- Verified: 47-check Playwright probe (7 unsound saves plus 1 sound resume, a recorded win with the right guess bucket, header pills, result-card text, a six-row chart, record surviving a reload, a loss resetting the streak, 6 corrupt-stats seeds, hard-mode counting with the green rule isolated, both media settings, answer not leaked before the end, advanceTime, page weight); HEAD-first confirmation of all four defects before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; test:corrupt-storage (100 games); test:reduced-motion (52 games); npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-27 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 12b: Cipher Rooms' first door punished you for following its own printed rule

Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the four rooms, their objects, clues, codes and scoring are unchanged.

- **The answer key was hiding in plain sight and the game never said so.** In every room the door runes are listed *in code order*: rune one is the symbol whose digit goes first. That relationship held in all four rooms and was stated nowhere — the "rule" line was flavour text about light levels and constellations.
- **And in the very first room the flavour text pointed the wrong way.** Moon Atrium said "Order symbols from low light to high light", its clue chips read circle 2, triangle 4, prism 7, and the door wanted 4-7-2. Measured on the shipped page: inspecting all three objects and entering **247 — exactly what the printed rule implies — is rejected**, costs 18 points, and burns one of only three attempts. On the first door of the game.
- **The door now shows the order it reads in.** Under the runes sits a numbered slot per position, each holding the digit you have discovered for that rune or a "?" until you find it. Discovering digits is still the entire puzzle; which slot each digit fills is no longer a guess. Verified: nothing is revealed before inspecting, one inspection fills exactly one slot, and reading the slots straight off the door opens all four doors first try and completes the run.
- **There were two different orders on one screen, which was the whole confusion.** The clue chips listed objects left to right while the door read its runes, and in room one those disagree. Chips are now numbered and ordered by the door, so exactly one order appears anywhere.
- **A load-time audit now proves the promise.** Every room's rune order is mapped through its own symbol-to-digit table and checked against its code; a mismatch says so on screen. Four of four rooms pass, so the rule the game now prints cannot quietly stop being true.
- **The keyboard was completely trapped.** Tab was swallowed to cycle objects, so eight real Tab presses never moved focus at all and every control after the scene was unreachable. Tab is handed back — the arrow keys already cycled objects — and arrows now step both directions instead of both stepping forward.
- **The loop ran forever on a puzzle where nothing moves.** Measured 61 frames per second on a fully idle room. It now parks unless a feedback message is decaying or a room transition is pending, verified at 0 idle frames while a message still decays in real time.
- **The stored best took anything.** `Infinity`, `-5`, `12.7` and `1e24` all reached the Best pill verbatim. Now required whole, non-negative and capped.
- **Joins the reduced-motion gate, 50 games to 51.** Another of the five games the 2026-08-16 census flagged as CSS-only; like memory-match it is DOM-rendered with no canvas, so its CSS coverage really is complete coverage — it simply never reported the state. Proved true under reduce and false under no-preference.
- Verified: 39-check Playwright probe (load-time audit of all four rooms, a full four-room win driven only by reading the door slots, slot reveal behaviour, chip-versus-slot order agreement, idle frames plus real-time feedback decay, a real six-press Tab walk, arrows both ways, 6 junk plus 1 valid storage seed with JSON validity each, both media settings, capture recipe verbatim, fresh-page determinism, page weight); HEAD-first confirmation of all six defects before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; test:reduced-motion (51 games); npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-27 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 12a: Pinball Foundry's biggest object was invisible, and most of its table could not be reached

Batch 12 opens (pinball-foundry, cipher-rooms, wordle, vector-pool, neon-drift, patchwork-foundry). Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the table art, the flipper geometry, the payout values and the ball-save rule are unchanged.

- **The table's largest object was drawn nowhere.** A 170px-radius deflector — 340px across, sitting over the whole top-centre of the table — reflects the ball and adds impulse, and there was not one pixel of it on screen. The ball visibly bounced off empty space. It is now drawn as the hood it always was, with its rim on the exact contact circle (collider radius plus ball radius), so what you see is where the ball actually turns. Verified by sampling canvas pixels on the rim, inside it, and outside it.
- **Two of the three bumpers, and the deflector itself, were physically unreachable.** The plunger topped out at 720px/s, which lifts the ball 474px — an apex of y=362 on a 980-tall table. A dense traffic map (50 balls across ten charge levels, autopiloted flippers, ~13,000 sampled frames) found that **0% of balls ever crossed y=200**, and the bumpers at y=260 and y=320 needed y≤308 and y≤364. Measured directly: **zero bumper hits and a score of zero across a full three-ball game**, and an apex of exactly y=391 on all 18 balls of a second run. The bumpers are the table's main scoring feature and the only thing that raises the multiplier.
- **The all-rollovers bonus was mathematically unearnable.** The first rollover needed the ball above y=356, inside that same dead zone, so "all three lit" — worth 600 plus a multiplier step — could never fire. Measured: zero rollovers lit across 18 balls.
- **The fix was measured, not eyeballed, and it was iterated four times.** A full plunger now clears the top wall, and the launch carries the ball across to the hood rather than straight up the chute — with a nearly-flat sideways drift only 15 of 50 balls touched a bumper, because the ball rode the lane up and came straight back down it. Targets were then placed in the corridors the map showed balls actually flying. Result: **all three bumpers struck (30/25/25 hits), all three rollovers struck, 45 of 50 balls hit a bumper, and the ball now reaches y=70.**
- **A first attempt at the rollovers made them farmable and was rejected.** Stacked as one vertical lane in the hottest column, a ball bouncing in place swept them repeatedly — measured 11 sweeps and 62,000 points in a single game against an old best of 8,750. They are now spread across three separate corridors, so a sweep needs the ball to visit the right lane, the centre and the left side.
- **The plunger silently dropped to zero while you were still holding it.** The charge ran `% 1`, so at about 1.4 seconds a full meter wrapped to 0% and holding "a good long time" produced the weakest possible launch. Nothing said so, and the help text implied the opposite. It now holds at 100%, and the charge label names what the power reaches: soft is low lanes only, medium is the rollover lane, strong is the upper bumpers, full reaches the hood.
- **The ball-save clock existed only in the diagnostics.** Six seconds decide whether a drain costs you a ball, and the player was told "ball save is lit" with no number. A Save pill now counts it down and agrees with the diagnostics clock to within 0.15s; it reads "used" once spent.
- **Not one scoring rule was stated anywhere.** Four bumpers buy a multiplier step, the cap is 5x, the multiplier multiplies every award, each side lane pays once only, and ball save lasts six seconds — all buried in the source. Every payout now runs through one shared constant block, printed both on the table and in the page text (canvas text at 14px shrinks to about 7px on a phone, so the table copy alone would leave phone players with nothing).
- **The stored best took anything.** `Infinity`, `-5`, `12.7` and `1e24` all reached the Best pill verbatim, and `1e24` printed as "1e+24" — an unbeatable record. Now required whole, non-negative and capped; a fractional value is rejected rather than rounded, because every award is a whole number times a whole multiplier.
- Verified: 41-check Playwright probe (per-target reachability over 42 balls at seven charges, apex measurement, charge-hold trace, all four charge labels, ball-save pill counting down against the diagnostics clock, canvas pixel sampling of the hood rim and the rules block, all rule constants reparsed from source, 6 junk plus 1 valid storage seed with JSON validity checked for each, capture recipe, fresh-page determinism, idle frames on the ready screen, a real five-press Tab walk, page weight); HEAD-first confirmation of every defect before any edit; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read at both sizes) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; test:reduced-motion (50 games, clean on re-run after one known transient); test:hidden-tab-clock; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-26 Claude — hidden-tab clock sweep: the catalog is clean, and now a gate keeps it that way

Follow-up to batch 11c, not a game pass. The memory-match fix closed a real defect — a run timer that charged the player's entire absence when they came back from another tab — but nothing said whether other games shared it, and nothing stopped the fix being deleted.

- **All 100 games were measured, not read.** A runtime census replaced `requestAnimationFrame` with a hand-pumped queue before any game script ran, drove each game into play, then delivered one frame 30 seconds late — exactly what a browser does after a tab switch, having sent no frames while hidden. **Zero games bill the absence.** memory-match was the only one, and it is fixed.
- **The detector was validated against a known-bad file before being believed.** Pointed at the pre-fix memory-match (served from git history, the repo untouched), it correctly reported the run clock charging 10,000 ms for a 10-second gap and 30,000 ms for a 30-second one — a clean 3x, worth 1,796 normal frames. Pointed at the current file it reports nothing. A detector that has not failed on purpose is not evidence.
- **The test is unit-free on purpose.** The first version compared the jump against a fixed threshold and reported typeforge-cipher as billing "33 seconds" — it was reading that game's **33 millisecond** dt clamp. Comparing a short gap against a long one and asking whether the bill scales removes the unit question entirely.
- **The 47 games the probe could not start were not counted as clean.** An unstarted game proves nothing, and calling it clean is exactly how a sweep silently under-covers. Every one was triaged at source: 28 have no frame-delta statement at all (turn-based board, word and quiz games), and the other 19 clamp. Five games looked unclamped to a source scan — flappy-bird, metro-dash, signal-siege, starline-strafe, shadow-switch — and all five turned out to be clamped, two on the line after the delta (flappy-bird's carries the comment "clamp to avoid big jumps after tab sleep") and three as the very first statement of the `update(dt)` they call. A regex cannot see a clamp that lives in the callee.
- **Why the rest of the catalog was already safe.** The clamp arrived as a side effect of the earlier double-speed campaign: every loop rewritten to use a real-elapsed accumulator got a `Math.min` with it. memory-match was never part of that sweep precisely *because* it was already real-elapsed — so it was the one game with the accumulator and none of the protection.
- **No game times a run off the wall clock either**, which would keep charging while hidden regardless of frames. Only two files compute an elapsed wall-clock span at all: a 2048 animation fraction clamped to 1, and the checkers AI's own search budget.
- **New gate: `npm run test:hidden-tab-clock`.** It hand-pumps frames for the 16 games measured to expose a real run clock and asserts that a frame arriving 30 seconds late charges no more than a clamped frame, and that tripling the absence does not triple the bill. It runs in the Validate Catalog browser-probe job, so a regression blocks the deploy gate. Proven to fail: with the pre-fix memory-match dropped back in, it fails with both the absolute charge and the scaling message, then passes again once restored.
- **The gate cannot silently cover nothing.** A game whose clock never advances is a **failure**, not a pass — the probe explicitly refuses to report a clean result for a game it did not manage to start. Building it caught three faults in itself and none in any game: it escalated its start actions past games that were already running (clicking Restart and difficulty buttons, zeroing the very clock it was about to measure), and it fired `pointerdown` as a bare `MouseEvent`, which makes any game calling `setPointerCapture` throw — reading as a game defect when it was the probe's.
- **Four games with a moving time field are deliberately excluded and named in the file**: switchback-rally's replay clock, tetris's drop accumulator, and the message-decay countdowns in pinball-foundry and cipher-rooms. A decay timer that skips ahead loses a banner early; it cannot cost a record.
- Verified: runtime census of all 100 games (positive and negative controls both passing); source triage of all 47 unstarted games plus hand-reading of the 5 apparent unclamped hits; wall-clock scan of all 100; the new gate passing three consecutive runs and failing on an injected regression; npm test 56/56; test:docs; test:test-aggregator; test:security-workflows; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-26 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 11c: Memory Match could not tell you whether a miss was your memory's fault, and billed your clock for time you were not playing

Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the three grids, the icon set, the scoring rule and the shuffle are untouched.

- **The clock billed a hidden tab for the entire time it was hidden.** The loop advanced `state.now` by the raw gap between animation frames, and a browser stops delivering frames to a background tab. Measured on the shipped page: one frame, a 30-second gap, then the next frame added **exactly 30,000ms to the run timer in a single step**. Since Best is decided on moves first and *time as the tiebreak*, an interrupted run lost a tiebreak it had already won. Fixed three ways that agree: frame gaps are clamped to 1 second, the loop stops on `visibilitychange` and re-arms with a fresh frame reference, and opening Help pauses the clock too. Verified: the same 30-second gap now charges only the clamp, the clock is frozen while hidden or while Help is open, and resumes within 450ms of coming back.
- **The game knew whether a miss was avoidable and never said so.** Memory is the entire skill, and the only feedback for a wrong pair was "Watch these two flip back" — identical whether you had flipped blind at two brand-new cards or had already seen that partner and forgotten it. The game now tracks which cards have been revealed, and a miss reads either "Two more cards learned. Flip on" (nothing to remember yet) or **"You had already seen the ⭐ partner."** A match splits the same way: "recalled from memory" versus "a lucky find". Cross-checked against an independent model over a scripted run: counters exact at 2 misses, 1 avoidable.
- **The mismatch lockout was pure dead time.** For 800ms after a wrong pair every click was silently swallowed — no message, nothing to learn, and it fires on most moves of most runs. A click during the lockout now resolves the stale pair instantly and flips the card you asked for, so the wait is never mandatory. Verified: the two stale cards flip back and the requested card is up in the same click.
- **A run could be destroyed by clicking the difficulty you were already on.** `setDifficulty` called `newGame()` unconditionally, so pressing the highlighted Easy button mid-run wiped the board with no confirmation. It now returns early when the level is unchanged; a real switch still starts fresh, as it should.
- **The board was 36 tab stops on hard.** Every card was individually tabbable and the container claimed `role="grid"` while no arrow key did anything — the role promised arrow navigation the game did not implement. Replaced with a single roving tab stop plus real arrow, Home and End movement, and the container role corrected to `group`. Verified with real key presses: one Tab enters the board, one Tab leaves it, and the arrow walk lands 1, 5, 4, 0, 15, 0 exactly as the 4-column geometry predicts.
- **Card labels named a number, not a place.** "Hidden card 14" tells a screen-reader user nothing about where it is; labels are now "Hidden card, row 4, column 2" and "⭐ card, matched, row 1, column 3".
- **The finish screen could not tell you how you did.** It showed moves and time against nothing. A perfect run is exactly the pair count, so the win card now carries a Minimum pill beside Moves, states the minimum in words, gives the miss postmortem ("2 misses, 1 where you had already seen the partner"), and when the run misses the record it names the standing best and the rule — fewest moves wins, ties go to time.
- **The stored best took anything JSON-shaped.** `{moves: 1e24, time: -5}` passed the typeof check and printed "1e+24" as an unbeatable record. Now rejected unless the moves count is a whole number at or above the pair count (a legal win cannot use fewer) and within a sane cap, and the time is finite and non-negative. Eight junk seeds rejected, a valid one kept.
- **A message existed in the source that no player could ever see.** "Checking the pair." was written on the second flip and immediately overwritten by the match or mismatch status inside the same call — measured as unreachable on the shipped page before removing it.
- **Joins the reduced-motion gate, taking it from 49 games to 50.** This was one of the five games named in the 2026-08-16 census as CSS-only. It is a DOM game, not a canvas one, so its CSS coverage really is complete coverage — but it reported no flag, so nothing verified that. It now reads the live media query and publishes it, proved true under reduce and false under no-preference.
- Verified: 57-check Playwright probe (both media settings, 8 junk plus 1 valid storage seed, a 30s hand-pumped frame gap, real Tab and arrow walks, a full 10-move winning run with an independent avoidable-miss model, click-through of the lockout, win-card text, help and hidden-tab clock pauses, `advanceTime` flip-back, capture recipe verbatim, page weight); scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read at both sizes) / audit:contrast (40 elements, 0 findings); full test:reduced-motion at 50 games; validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean. Checked and already correct: the loop already parks (0 frames before the first flip and after the win), `advanceTime` still resolves a pending flip-back deterministically, and the capture recipe still lands a real match.

## 2026-08-20 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 11b: Typeforge Cipher measured danger in pixels while the player paid in seconds

Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the wave generator, the fragment alphabet and the scoring curve are sound and byte-identical, and a perfect-play bot still finishes all six waves for the same 109,989.

- **The danger warning shrank exactly as the danger grew.** Fragments fall faster every wave (67px/s in wave 1 up to 112px/s in wave 6) but the red "about to leak" tint fired at a **fixed 86 pixels** above the fail line. Measured from the constants: that is **1.28 seconds of warning in wave 1 and 0.77 in wave 6**, a 40% cut — while the penalty for a leak rises the other way, from 11 integrity to 16, a 45% rise. The player's reaction window was quietly halved and nothing said so.
- **Danger is now measured in seconds.** One pure `tokenRisk()` returns the real seconds until a fragment crosses the fail line, and the thresholds are typing-time based rather than pixel based: amber under 2.2s (finish what you are typing, then come here), red under 1.0s (the longest fragment is 5 glyphs, so this is still savable if you are already in the column). The band now means the same thing in every wave.
- **The whole decision of the game had no surface at all.** With four columns and several fragments in flight, the only real question is *which column needs me next* — and the game already sorted exactly that internally and showed none of it. Every lane now carries a live countdown under it, the soonest one is marked NEXT with an arrow, and the DOM status says it in words for phones. Cross-checked against an independently rebuilt model: **72 column countdowns and 1,046 urgency samples, zero mismatches**.
- **The biggest penalty was the only one that never named its cost.** A bad glyph said "Bad glyph -6 integrity", a bad submit said "-5" — but a leak, the largest drain and the only one that scales, said just "Leak in column 3". It now names the fragment and the exact cost, verified live at waves 1, 3 and 6 as -11, -13 and -16.
- **Nothing announced that the game speeds up.** Each wave now opens with "Wave 4 armed. Fragments fall 94px/s (up from 85). A leak costs 14 integrity." — every number recomputed from the same functions the simulation uses, checked on all six briefings.
- **A total keyboard trap, and the worst one found in this campaign.** Tab was swallowed unconditionally to cycle columns, so **ten real Tab presses never moved focus at all** — Sound, Fullscreen and all four column buttons were unreachable by keyboard, with no Shift+Tab escape. Tab is handed back to the browser; arrows and the 1-4 keys already cycled columns, so nothing was lost. Verified with real key presses: the walk now reaches every control.
- **A deliberate restraint on the new text.** The status line sits inside an `aria-live` region, so the countdown there is **level-based, not a ticking number** — a per-frame count would talk over a screen reader continuously. The exact seconds live on the canvas, where a changing number costs nothing. Measured: 2 changes over 2 seconds instead of 60.
- **The stored best had no ceiling.** `1e24` survived the finite-and-positive guard and printed "1e+24", unbeatable forever. Capped at 250,000, safely above the arithmetic ceiling of ~197,000 for a 72-fragment run.
- **A pre-existing capture-recipe defect fixed too.** The recipe named "start a wave and type the active token" advanced only 400ms while the first fragment spawns at 720ms, so it typed into an empty board; and it read the globally lowest fragment's text while focus stayed on column 1, so once a token did exist the keystrokes hit "No token" and **drained integrity instead of typing**. Confirmed present on the previous commit before changing it. It now focuses the lane holding the fragment and types the game's own active token, scoring 343 at full integrity.
- Verified: 32-check Playwright probe (full six-wave winning run cross-checked against an independently rebuilt wave/risk model, live leak costs at three waves, real 10-press Tab walk, canvas pixel sampling of the countdown strip, 5 junk plus 2 valid storage seeds, fresh-page determinism, hand-pumped synthetic-clock runs at 60Hz and 120Hz agreeing to 9ms and to the pixel, aria-live churn, capture recipe verbatim, phone status text); scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read at both sizes) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean. Checked and already correct: the loop is already gated (0 frames on the ready screen) and already real-elapsed, so there is no double-speed defect.

## 2026-08-20 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 11a: Bloomkeeper Grid judged you on tomorrow's garden while showing you today's

Batch 11 opens (bloomkeeper-grid, typeforge-cipher, memory-match, switchback-rally, inkline-courier, packet-pilot). Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the five stage layouts, goals and scoring curve are sound, and the STAGES literal is byte-identical.

- **The check runs on tomorrow's board, but every surface showed today's.** Pressing Day grows the garden first and judges the goals after, yet the goal chips, the canvas panel and the status line all reported the pre-growth state. The trap is real and one move deep: planting a seed next to a sprinkler makes an instant sprout, the "1/1 yellow sprout" chip turns green, and the very next Day press ages that sprout into a bloom and un-readies the goal — on the last day that is a loss the interface promised was a win. Measured on the shipped page before the fix: chip `1/1`, one Day press, `0/1`.
- **One pure `dayOutlook()` now simulates the next Day press through the same `derivePlantIn()` the real press runs**, and every surface reads it: each goal chip carries `Day 2 → 2/2`, the chip's green now means "will be satisfied when checked", the canvas goals panel shows the same forecast, the idle status line is the persistent verdict ("Day 1 grows 2/2 red bloom · 1/1 yellow sprout · ready +429"), and the diagnostics publish the whole record. Verified across a full five-stage winning run: 9 Day presses, every per-goal prediction matched reality exactly, and every promised completion bonus equalled the score actually awarded.
- **The primary button could end the run with no warning.** Pressing Day on the final day with goals short fails the garden, and nothing said so. The Day button now turns red with an aria-label naming the stake when the forecast says the press loses, driven by the same outlook.
- **The watering field was invisible exactly where planning happens.** Water state was only stored on planted seeds, so empty soil — the squares you are choosing between — showed nothing, and the only marker (a wave circle) also missed the outermost ring of real reach. Measured: a watered empty cell was pixel-identical to a dry one (rgb delta 0.7). The board now computes the field live from the same distance rule the growth uses: a blue wash on every currently watered square, a dashed edge on the exact ring the next Day press adds, and a purple corner tick on every square beside shade.
- **The growth rules were never stated.** The entire system — water reach grows one square a day, sprout on the first watered day, bloom after two, leaf when shade sits beside (which overrides bloom), star at water + shade + three days, dry days wilt plants back — appeared nowhere; the canvas offered "Water makes blooms. Shade makes leaves." The exact recipes are now on the canvas panel and, because 12px canvas text dies on a phone, in a DOM help line as well. Placing a seed also teaches the timing: "Seed planted. Water arrives on day 1."
- **Every stage proven winnable by construction.** An independent reimplementation of the rules validated a hand-built plan for all five stages within the move and day budgets, and the probe then played those plans through the real page: run complete, score 2169, every plant matching the independent model on every day of the run.
- **Refusals name their reason now.** Placing on a failed, completed, or finished garden and pressing N in those states each answered with the same silence or a wrong message ("No moves left" on a completed stage); all now say what is true, including that clearing a piece costs a move.
- **The stored best took anything.** `Infinity`, `-5`, `12.7` and `1e24` all reached the Best pill verbatim (measured). Now guarded finite, non-negative, whole, and capped at 5000 — just above the theoretical five-stage maximum — with junk-reject and valid-keep seeds both proven.
- Also: the canvas stat line no longer runs underneath the board slab (counts stack in the left margin, and the duplicated tool name is gone — the HTML pill already shows it), and the stage title cleared the slab too.
- Verified: 39-check Playwright probe (full five-stage win with outlook-vs-actual on all 9 Day presses and independent-model cross-check on every plant, trap replay, final-day warning, fail/complete refusal texts, water/ring/shade pixel sampling with surface-derived thresholds, 6 junk + 2 valid storage seeds, reload persistence, fresh-page determinism, real Tab walk, capture recipe verbatim, idle frames 0, phone layout); scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots eyeballed at both sizes) / audit:contrast (0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-16 Claude — reduced-motion coverage: 36 protected games to 49, found by measuring instead of reading the list

The lumen-lander pass ended by noticing that a game can honour `prefers-reduced-motion` and still sit outside the catalog's gate, because the gate is a hand-maintained list. That is a silent coverage hole: nothing stops a listed behaviour from being deleted in a game nobody remembered to list. This pass measured the real state of all 100 games and closed the part of the hole that could be closed safely.

- **The census was a runtime one, not a grep.** Every game was loaded twice, once under `reduce` and once under `no-preference`, and asked what `render_game_to_text()` actually reports. Greps over the source were wrong in both directions here, so the count that matters is the one the browser gives.
- **Twelve games already did everything right and were simply never listed**: aster-vault, blackjack, chromalock, dungeon-circuit, eclipse-grid, floodgate, flux-reversi, fourfall, gemline-cascade, rune-roster, seedline, shard-sheriff. They reported the live media state correctly and loaded clean under both settings. Adding them required **no game changes at all**, and it took the gate from 36 to 48.
- **One more needed a single line.** `pylon-shift` already stilled its sparks, screen shake, confetti, disc squash and idle bob behind a live `matchMedia` read; it just never reported the flag, so it could not be gated. Exposing it took the gate to **49**.
- **Zero games were found reporting the flag incorrectly**, and none of the 36 already-listed games were failing. The hole was purely one of listing, not of behaviour.
- **The distinction that matters, and that a grep gets wrong.** 54 games mention `prefers-reduced-motion`, but five of the remaining six do it **only in CSS** — beacon-bastion, cipher-rooms, idle-tycoon, memory-match and reflex-spark ship the shared stylesheet reset that neutralises CSS animations and transitions. That does nothing for motion painted onto a canvas, which is where these games' flashes and sparks live. They are **not** one-line additions and were deliberately not added: giving them real coverage means implementing the JS read and gating the canvas motion, which is per-game work.
- **What is left, stated as candidates rather than findings.** 51 games do not report the flag. A source scan flagged 24 of them as containing words like shake, particle, flash, spark, trail or burst, and 21 with no such marker. Those numbers are **not verified findings** — the scan cannot tell a screen shake from a variable called `flashMessage`, and it cannot see motion built from unnamed literals. The honest statement is that up to 24 games are worth reading by hand, in that order, and each one that genuinely paints decorative motion is a real accessibility gap rather than a bookkeeping one.
- **A catalog quirk confirmed as intentional, not a defect.** The census first reported 99 games because the manifest slug `solitaire` points at a file named `solitare.html`. The manifest's `url` field carries the real path and every gate resolves it correctly; the frozen-catalog policy forbids renaming the file. Recorded so the next census does not re-raise it.
- Verified: runtime census of all 100 games under both motion settings (200 page loads, console and page errors captured for each); `npm run test:reduced-motion` passing at 49 games, up from 36, with every added game proved by the gate itself to report `true` under reduce and `false` under no-preference; scoped test:games and capture:games for the one touched game (desktop and mobile both 0); test:catalog-freeze; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-16 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 10f: Lumen Lander never told you the gravity, and flew at 1.9x on a fast screen

Batch 10 closes here (paddle-pulse, bulwark-burst, deckforge-duel, nightwire, slipstream-sprint, lumen-lander). Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the four stage layouts, the terrain, the pad placement and the scoring curve are all sound, and the stage table is byte-identical.

- **This game was already good at disclosure, which is what made the gap sharp.** The safe landing limits are printed in the header, in the status line, on the canvas, and colour-coded on two pills. The one number that was never shown anywhere is **gravity, and it is the only one that changes** — `36`, `41`, `46`, `52` across the four stages. Measured before the fix: the word "gravity" and every one of those four values appeared in exactly zero of the page text, the canvas draw calls, and the diagnostics. The player relearned each stage by crashing.
- **Gravity is now stated three ways and the change is announced.** The canvas carries `GRAVITY 46  THRUST 94  BURN 12/S  8.8S LEFT`, the diagnostics report it, and each stage opens with a briefing that names the number **and where it came from**: "Crater Relay. Gravity 41, up from 36. Fuel 132 is 11.0s of burn." Verified by flying and landing all four stages with a scripted pilot and checking every surface on each.
- **The Fuel pill was a number with no unit of meaning.** Fuel `118` is really `9.8` seconds of burn at 12 a second, and thrust only beats gravity while it lasts. The pill now reads `118 · 9.8s`, and the probe proves one second of held thrust really costs exactly the stated rate.
- **The pills rounded an illegal landing down into a legal-looking number.** The old readout used `Math.round`, so a real drop of `48.2` against a limit of `48` printed **"48"** — the exact limit, so a pilot reading the pill concluded they were legal and the pad rejected them. Caught on a real frame before the fix. Now rounded **up** against an upper limit, so a shown number at or under the limit is always genuinely legal.
- **The old velocity format collided with the limits themselves.** It printed `34/48` for sideways-over-vertical, and 34 and 48 *are* the two limits, so it read as "34 out of 48". Axes are named now: `H15 V9`.
- **The drop test is signed, and the pill did not know that.** The rule is `vy <= 48`, so a craft still **rising** has no vertical problem at all, but the pill tested `|vy| > 48` and flashed red on a fast climb. Both now read from one pure `landingCheck()` that `contact()` also decides with. Sampled every frame of three full descents: **0 disagreements**.
- **A rejection now names the test that failed.** "Pad rejected speed or tilt" became "Pad rejected: drop 55 over 48."
- **The game ran at 1.90x speed on a 120Hz display.** The loop spent `max(1, round(elapsed / 16.67))` fixed slices per frame, so a 120Hz screen got a whole 1/60 slice every 8.3ms. Measured over 2 seconds of real wall clock: vertical speed **80 at 60Hz versus 152 at 120Hz**, position 181 versus 413. A landing tuned on a normal screen was unflyable on a fast one. Replaced with a real accumulator that banks elapsed milliseconds and carries the remainder. Re-measured: 60Hz, 120Hz and 144Hz now agree to within 0.6.
- **The stored best took anything.** `Infinity`, `-40` and `1e24` all loaded and printed straight onto the Best pill, the last unbeatable forever. Guarded for finiteness, direction and a ceiling; all 12 junk seeds clamp and both valid seeds still load.
- **One accessibility gate grew.** The game already honoured reduced motion for its crash flash but never exposed the flag, so it sat outside the catalog's reduced-motion check. It now reports the live state and joins the gate, taking it from 35 covered games to 36.
- Verified: 32-assertion Playwright probe including a scripted pilot that lands **all four stages** (so the per-stage gravity checks cannot pass vacuously on stage 1), every sentence's numbers recomputed from the constants rather than matched, pills cross-checked against the landing rule on every frame of three descents, synthetic-clock runs at 60/120/144Hz, 12 junk plus 2 valid storage seeds, a real Tab walk, and a phone-width run proving the dropped canvas text is still carried by the DOM; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read at both sizes) / audit:contrast (56 elements, 0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; test:corrupt-storage; test:reduced-motion (36 games); test:best-score-guard; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-15 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 10e: the slipstream the game is named after was never drawn

Batch 10 continues (paddle-pulse, bulwark-burst, deckforge-duel, nightwire, slipstream-sprint, lumen-lander). Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the lap layout, the seven-event seed pattern, the speed model and the scoring curve are all sound, and the event seed table is byte-identical.

- **The mechanic in the title existed only inside a filter expression.** Drafting pays `+28` boost a second, but only while a rival sits **between 75 and 205 back in your own lane**, and only while you are **not boosting**. None of that was drawn, labelled, or stated. The one signal, a status message, was gated behind `if (state.feedbackTimer <= 0)` — measured over a full race, the player was genuinely drafting on **30 frames and the screen said so on 2 of them (7%)**. The other 93% it showed unrelated text such as "Barrier hit. Integrity down."
- **The race even opens inside the slipstream and never says so.** The first seeded rival sits 150 back in the centre lane, which is exactly where the player starts, so boost begins climbing on the first frame for a reason the player cannot see anywhere on screen.
- **Both zones turned out to be fixed horizontal strips on screen**, because both are defined purely by the gap to the player and the player never leaves `PLAYER_Y`. That is what made them drawable at all. The slipstream is now a dashed-railed strip in the player's own lane that fills lime and reads "DRAFT +28 BOOST/S" while it is paying, turns gold and reads "BOOST CANCELS DRAFT" when boost is cancelling it, and pink ticks at the player's shoulders mark exactly where contact begins. The free margins either side of the road now carry the draft rule and what boost actually buys.
- **A phone cannot read canvas text, so the words moved to the DOM.** The 960x540 backing store scales to about 0.40 on a phone, which puts 11px labels under 5px. Below the stylesheet's own breakpoint the margin panels and the in-lane label are dropped and the rails thicken; the strip carries the rule as shape and colour, and the **Boost pill reads `58 +28/s` in lime** exactly when the draft is charging. Verified on all 246 frames of scripted play.
- **The boost button used to lie.** Engaging needed `boost > 2` while a clean pass needed `boost > 3`, so at 2.5 the button lit, the flame drew, and you crashed into the rival anyway. Both now share one `BOOST_MIN`, and boost disengages **after** the drain, so "the button is lit" always implies "this pass will be clean". Measured: 0 frames boosting at or below the threshold, 0 rival collisions while boosting.
- **Only a win banked the score.** A crash threw the run away and so did Restart — measured, a 579-point run ending in a breach left Best at 0, and a mid-race restart binned 72 points. Every exit banks now. The stored best was also uncapped and undirected: `Infinity`, `-40` and `1e24` all printed straight onto the Best pill, the last of them unbeatable forever. All 14 junk seeds now clamp and both valid seeds still load.
- **The finish added thousands of points in silence.** Three bonuses (time, integrity, boost left) were summed with nothing on screen saying so. The finish now prints the whole sum, and the probe checks the printed parts add up to the score actually awarded.
- **A second animation chain was running.** `loop()` zeroes its frame handle before calling `update()`, and `update()` calls `setFeedback()`, which re-arms — so the game ran two chains at once. Measured **253 frames per 2s, now 121**. Ready screen 0, settled race 0, and the race clock still advances 1.50s in 1.50s of real time.
- **Open finding, deliberately not fixed: the drawn cars are bigger than the rules they represent.** A car is 68px tall, which at this perspective is **124 distance units, while the entire contact window is only 104**. Measured, a rival's body starts overlapping the player's at a gap of about 133 — well inside the legal draft band — so the sprites appear to touch while the rules say you are safely drafting. Fixing it needs either a steeper perspective (which cuts the visible warning distance from 712 units to 392, roughly halving reaction time) or squashing every car to 54% height, which is a redesign of the game's main sprite and outside a repair pass. The drawn strip and the contact ticks now carry the truth instead; the sprite overlap is cosmetic, not a rules trap.
- Verified: 31-assertion Playwright probe including an independent draft model parsed straight out of the file and cross-checked on all 246 frames of scripted play (0 mismatches, all six draft phases exercised), rail pixels sampled at the exact band the rule uses (player lane 0.61 lit versus empty lanes 0.00), 14 junk plus 2 valid storage seeds, real 60Hz frame counts for idle/racing/settled, a real Tab walk landing on Sound, and every number in the help sentence recomputed from the constants rather than merely matched; scoped test:games / capture:games (desktop and mobile both 0, screenshots read at both sizes and in every draft state) / audit:contrast (42 elements, 0 findings); validate-catalog; test:catalog-freeze; test:corrupt-storage; test:reduced-motion; test:best-score-guard; npm test 56/56; git diff --check clean.

## 2026-08-15 Claude — catalog-wide sweep for the Nightwire defect: a hidden cost the player is never shown

Nightwire's fault was **not** the Deckforge one. Deckforge printed one number and applied another; Nightwire printed **no number at all** — the constants that decided whether you lived were applied every turn and never reached a pixel. All 100 games were swept for that separate class. No code changed in this pass; this entry records what was checked, how the checker was proved to work, and what was found.

- **The detector was validated before its result was believed.** A scan that reports nothing is worthless unless it demonstrably fires on the real thing, so it was run against the **pre-fix Nightwire** pulled out of git history. It fired on both shapes: the hidden `34` cost on the capped alarm, and the silent `4`-vs-`3` vision switch. It is clean on post-fix Nightwire. That is what makes the zeros below mean something.
- **Shape A, a hidden cost on a resource that can kill you: 0 games.** A resource with a failure check, something adding or subtracting a fixed constant, and neither the cost nor the room left ever shown. Checked in **both directions** — a meter climbing to a cap like Nightwire's alarm, and health or fuel falling to a floor, which is how most of the catalog kills you. Checking only the rising shape would have declared the catalog clean while missing every game that kills by subtraction.
- **Shape B, a rule that silently changes at a threshold: 0 games.** A constant compared against a resource whose result switches between two other constants, with none of the three displayed — Nightwire's cones growing from 3 tiles to 4 at alarm 50.
- **Shape C, one action billed twice: 2 games, both already handled.** `chrome-convoy`'s damage function has three real call sites and none can fire twice for one action: a rammed rival is destroyed, a slammed blocker is pushed out of the collision window, and the edge scrape is gated by a half-second timer. `nightwire`'s alarm function genuinely can bill one step twice, which is the rule this campaign found and wrote into its help card in batch 10d.
- **One false positive, and it was instructive.** `gridline-tactics` first flagged for switching between `SHOT_DMG` 3 and `SHOT_DMG_COVERED` 2. It is in fact a **model citizen**: it carries a comment saying "Every tactical number lives here once… so the board can never advertise a rule the game does not actually follow", and its plan bar prints *"A crate in the way drops the shot from 3 to 2."* — the same fix shape this campaign has been applying, already done. My checker missed it because it only looked at lines that also contained a text-drawing call, and gridline builds its explanation into a returned record that a different line draws. The check was widened to count any string that names the constant, then **re-validated against pre-fix Nightwire** to prove the loosening had not gone too far.
- **What this sweep can and cannot see.** It reads the source, so it finds a hidden rule only where a named constant is applied to a resource the code visibly fails on. It would miss a cost computed inline from unnamed literals, a rule that lives in the shape of a data table rather than a constant, or a number disclosed only in an image. The conclusion is that **no other instance of the Nightwire defect was found**, not that none can exist.
- Verified: the detector proved on three known cases (a known positive, a known negative, and a known false positive) before and after being widened, then run over all 100 games, with both remaining hits read by hand in the source.

## 2026-08-14 Claude — overhaul campaign batch 10d: Nightwire never told you how many mistakes you had

Batch 10 continues (paddle-pulse, bulwark-burst, deckforge-duel, nightwire, slipstream-sprint, lumen-lander). Targeted pass, not a rewrite — the five stage maps, the guard patrols, the vision cones and the takedown rules are all sound, and all five map literals are byte-identical.

- **The whole tension of the game lived in four constants and none of them reached a pixel.** A sighting adds `34` to a `100` alarm, so the **third one catches you**. At `50` the guards go alert, which silently stretches **every vision cone from 3 tiles to 4**. A quiet turn pays back only `4`. The Alarm pill showed a bare percentage and changed colour, which says something is wrong but never says how much room is left, and the cones grew without a word.
- **The budget is now visible in three places.** The pill reads `68% · 0 left`. A meter stands in the free margin beside the board, filling from the bottom, divided once per sighting it can still take, with a dashed line marking where the cones grow, and `0 LEFT` beneath it. The help card states all four numbers.
- **My own first wording was the exact defect this campaign is chasing, and the probe caught it.** I wrote "you survive three", but `34 × 3` is `102`, which is past the cap — you survive **two** and the third catches you. Corrected everywhere, and the probe now checks that claim as arithmetic on the constants rather than trusting the sentence.
- **A second fault of mine, also caught by measuring.** I put the alert notice inside the alarm-raising function, but every caller follows that with its own `Spotted!` message, so the notice was wiped before a frame was drawn. The escalation is now recorded as a flag and folded into the one message the player actually sees: `Spotted! Alert: every cone now reaches 4 tiles.`
- **A rule worth stating that the game never stated.** Stepping into a cone is charged **twice in one turn** — once on the step, and again at the end of the guards' turn if you are still standing in it. Measured directly: one careless step took the alarm from `0` to `68`. The help text and the pill's tooltip now say so.
- **Checked against reality, not just against itself.** The record was compared over **260 consecutive steps** to a model written separately from constants read out of the file — **zero mismatches** on every field including the published guard vision, which tracked the alert state on all 260 frames. Then the world was run forward: every alarm rise was a whole number of sightings at the published cost, the run ended exactly at the cap, and the meter's fill really moved when the alarm rose.
- **The stored best was the weak read again.** `Number(v) || 0` let `Infinity`, `-40`, `12.7` and `1e24` through. Now finite, whole, non-negative and capped: **nine junk records rejected, three genuine records kept exactly**. Restart now banks the score first, where before it wiped it.
- **Checked and already correct, stated as findings not fixes.** The loop was already gated and stays gated: the menu costs **0** frames and a turn-based board at rest costs **0**. The same input script gives the same result on a fresh page. A real five-press Tab walk reaches Restart, Sound, Fullscreen and Help untrapped. Both storage keys are unrenamed, and the five stage maps the solvability gate parses are byte-identical.
- **Left alone on purpose:** the meter shows your own alarm budget and says nothing about where any guard will look next. The id `touchLayer` is declared and never referenced again, which predates this pass and is cosmetic.
- **A probe fault worth recording.** My first walk cycled right, down, left, up — which returns to the same square every four presses, so the agent never met a guard and two tests reported a game fault that was really a walk going nowhere. Direction cycles used to explore must have net movement.
- Verified: a 23-assertion Playwright probe covering all of the above, plus desktop and phone screenshots with no sideways scroll at either size. Plus the grid-solvability gate, the capture recipe, scoped `test:games`, `capture:games` (desktop and mobile both **0**), `audit:contrast` clean, catalog validation, catalog freeze, `npm test` 56/56, and `git diff --check` clean.
